So I end up running a little behind, or I get there kind of flustered. And even during appointments, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether they’re looking at that part of my face instead of fully listening to what they’re saying, which is not great in my line of work.\n\nAnd socially, yeah, a bit. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and there have been a few recently that I just didn’t go to because I didn’t want to be seen, or I was worried someone would take pictures and post them somewhere. I know that probably sounds excessive, because no one has actually said anything to me, but in the moment it feels easier to just avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or on your phone a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number exactly, no. It’s not like I have to do it five times or something or I can’t leave. It’s more… I don’t feel settled until I’ve checked it enough that it looks “okay,” and the problem is “okay” keeps changing depending on the light or the angle.\n\nLike before client meetings, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then I’ll pull up my phone camera because somehow that feels more honest, but then I’ll adjust the lighting or tilt my head a little and it looks different again. Then I’ll mess with my collar, or try to angle my face so that side of my jaw doesn’t look as noticeable. It can become several checks pretty quickly.\n\nAnd even after I stop, it’s not always because I feel reassured. Sometimes it’s just because I’m out of time. Then when I’m with people, I catch myself comparing my face to theirs — like younger advisors, or people at gallery events — and I’m thinking about whether their jawline looks tighter or more even instead of focusing on the actual conversation. It’s embarrassing to say, but yeah, it’s become kind of a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel this way about your face if everyone around you, including strangers, told you that you look fine and there's nothing wrong with your jawline?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I think I probably would still feel it, yeah. Maybe I’d be a little reassured for a few minutes if everyone said, “No, you look fine,” but that’s kind of already what happens with my wife, and even clients don’t seem to notice anything. Nobody’s pointing at my jaw or acting weird. But I still get stuck on it.\n\nIt’s like once I saw it in those family photos — that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially on one side of my face — I couldn’t stop seeing it. So even if strangers told me it was fine, part of me would think they were just being polite, or they didn’t see the angle I’m seeing. I know that sounds irrational, but to me it looks really obvious when I catch it the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social events like art openings, where this concern about your face is affecting your ability to participate or enjoy activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… besides those things, it’s mostly those same areas spilling over into everything else. Like, I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life completely. I’m still going to work, I’m still meeting with clients, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do. But it kind of colors things more than I want it to.\n\nThe biggest concrete things are what I mentioned — getting out the door has become slower, especially before client meetings, because I’m checking how my face looks and trying to make sure that side of my jaw isn’t too obvious. And then even when I’m actually in the appointment, I’m sometimes less present than I should be, because part of my mind is on whether they’re noticing it.\n\nAnd with my wife, the art openings are the clearest example. We used to go to those pretty regularly, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or I was worried there’d be photos. So it’s not like I’m avoiding everything, but I’m definitely choosing not to participate in some things I normally would have enjoyed.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern more about this specific issue with your jawline or are you worried that you're just unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more the specific jawline thing, I think. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking every part of me is ugly or that I’m just generally unattractive. It’s really this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face that I keep getting stuck on.\n\nIt started feeling much worse after I saw myself in those family photos. I kept noticing that one side looked heavier or less defined, and now it’s like my eye goes straight there. My wife says she barely notices it, and obviously clients aren’t saying anything, but that doesn’t really stop me from seeing it.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s a general “I hate my whole face” thing. It’s more like this one feature has become the thing I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline actually is uneven, or are you more afraid that it might become uneven or be perceived that way by others?\n\nPatient: I think… to me it actually looks uneven. It’s not just that I’m worried it might happen someday or that people might imagine it. When I look at myself, especially in certain angles or lighting, I see this sagging, kind of uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nAnd that really started after those family photos. I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s like once I saw it there, I couldn’t unsee it. Before that, maybe I had normal concerns here and there, but those pictures made it feel very real to me. Now when I look in the mirror or see a photo, my attention goes right to that area.\n\nAt the same time, I know other people don’t seem to react to it the way I do. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients aren’t saying anything or acting like something is wrong. So I guess the honest answer is: I believe I’m seeing something uneven, but I’m also very caught up in whether other people can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jawline were somehow resolved?\n\nPatient: I think I would feel a lot better, yes. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of everything wrong with me. It’s this one thing that keeps pulling my attention back.\n\nSo if somehow that were resolved — if I could look in the mirror or see a photo and not immediately see that unevenness — I think I’d probably feel much more comfortable. Especially because this all seemed to get worse after seeing myself in those family photos. Since then, I just keep noticing it, even though my wife says she barely sees anything and clients don’t seem to notice.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I’d never find something else to worry about, but honestly, right now it feels like if this jawline issue weren’t there, I’d be much more satisfied with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your jawline being uneven is based on how you actually look, or is it more about how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it feels like it’s based on how I actually look, because when I see it, I really see it — that sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s not just an abstract feeling of “I don’t like myself” or something. My eye goes right there, especially in photos or certain angles.\n\nBut then I also have to admit that other people don’t seem to be seeing it the same way. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t reacted like there’s anything unusual. So that makes me wonder if part of it is how strongly I feel about it, or how locked onto it I’ve become.\n\nIt really got worse after those family photos. I saw myself in them and just kept thinking, “That side of my face looks heavier, my jawline looks off.” Since then, it’s like I can’t look at myself without checking that area. So… I guess I’d say I believe there’s something there, but I’m probably reacting to it much more intensely than other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are bothered more by the fact that you believe your jawline is currently uneven, or by the fear that it could become uneven in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more the fact that I believe it’s uneven now. I’m not really sitting around thinking, “What if my jawline becomes uneven years from now?” That’s not the thing. What brings me in is that when I look at myself right now, I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nAnd it’s especially tied to those family photos. I know I’ve mentioned them a lot, but that was really when it jumped out at me. Since then, it’s like every mirror, every picture, certain lighting — my eyes go right there. I start comparing one side to the other, thinking one side looks heavier or lower or just… off.\n\nSo the fear part is more about whether other people can see what I’m seeing, I guess. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t said anything or acted differently, which should probably reassure me more than it does. But the main thing bothering me is that I feel like I can see it already. It feels current, not hypothetical.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your jawline being uneven is something that you see in your reflection, or is it something you feel or sense is there, even if others don't notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly something I see, or at least I feel like I see it, when I’m looking at my reflection or especially in photos. It’s not just a vague sense, like I wake up and “feel” my jaw is uneven without looking. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nThe family photos really set it off, honestly. I saw myself in all those pictures, and it was like suddenly that area looked so obvious to me — like one side was lower or heavier. Since then, when I look in the mirror, my eyes go straight there. I’ll turn my face a little or look from another angle, and I’m trying to see if it’s as noticeable as it looked in the photos.\n\nBut the confusing part is that my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice it either. So I guess it’s both: I’m seeing it in my reflection and pictures, but maybe I’m also sensing it or focusing on it more than other people do. It feels real to me, though. I can’t just unsee it once I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even when you're not looking in a mirror or at photos?\n\nPatient: Not in the same way, no. I mean, when I’m not looking at myself, I’m not literally seeing it, obviously. But it’s still kind of there in my mind, if that makes sense. Like I can be sitting at my desk or talking to someone, and I’ll have this awareness of that side of my face, like, “Is it looking saggy right now? Can they see it from this angle?”\n\nBut the strongest reaction is definitely when I see myself — in a mirror, or worse, in photos. Those family photos really set something off for me. Since then, it’s like I keep returning to this idea that my jawline looks uneven, especially one side of my face, and I have trouble letting it go even though my wife says she barely notices it and clients don’t seem to react to it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t exactly “see” it when I’m not looking, but I’m still preoccupied with it. It stays in my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your jawline something that you think would be resolved if you could just change that one feature, or do you think it's part of a larger issue with how you feel about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I want to say it would be resolved if I could just fix that one thing, because that’s honestly where my mind goes. Like, if my jawline just looked even again, or if that one side of my face didn’t look saggy or heavier to me, I feel like I’d be able to stop thinking about it so much.\n\nBut… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate it out, because right now it feels like it’s all centered on that feature. What brings me in is really that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. Especially after those family photos — that was when it really hit me. Since then, it’s like I can’t look at myself without checking that area first.\n\nMy wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t said anything or seemed to react, so part of me knows maybe changing that one thing wouldn’t magically fix everything. But emotionally, it feels like if that part looked right, I’d feel much more comfortable. I don’t think I’m sitting here hating every part of my appearance in general. It’s more like this one thing has become the thing I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your jawline if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I think… probably less, but I don’t think it would completely disappear. If I were truly never seeing myself — no mirrors, no photos, nothing reflective — then maybe it wouldn’t hit me as hard, because the photos and mirrors are what really make it flare up. Especially those family photos. That was the point where I got stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nBut even if I were alone, I think the idea of it would still be in my head. Like I’d still know, or worry, that it’s there. I’d probably wonder, “If someone did see me, would they notice that one side looks heavier?” So it’s not only about other people’s reactions, because honestly my wife says she barely notices anything and clients don’t seem to notice it either. It’s more that once I saw it, I couldn’t stop focusing on it.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think being alone would fully solve it. It might make it quieter, but I’d probably still be preoccupied with whether my jawline looks uneven.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jawline is the main reason you worry about your appearance, or is it part of a larger fear of being unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly the jawline. At least that’s what it feels like to me right now. It’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m just generally unattractive,” across the board. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nIt really got worse after seeing myself in all those family photos. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s kind of the image that keeps replaying in my mind. Since then, when I think about my appearance, my attention goes straight there first — that one side looking heavier or off somehow.\n\nMy wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything either, which should probably reassure me more than it does. But it doesn’t fully. So I wouldn’t say it’s a broad fear that I’m unattractive in every way. It’s more like this one feature has become the thing that makes me worry about my appearance overall.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even when others tell you it doesn't and you're not looking at any reflections of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not as strongly as when I’m actually looking in the mirror or at a photo, but the thought is still there even when I’m not seeing it in front of me.\n\nIt’s like once I noticed it — especially after those family photos — I can’t really unsee it. Even if my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t react or say anything, my mind still kind of holds onto this image of one side of my jaw or face looking heavier, saggy, uneven. So if I’m just sitting there, not looking at any reflection, I can still picture it and feel bothered by it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what other people are telling me, and I don’t mean to. I hear them when they say it’s not noticeable. But internally, I still feel like it’s there. It’s just quieter when I’m not actively checking or seeing myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline actually looks uneven to you, even when nobody else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think it looks uneven to me, even when nobody else seems to notice it. That’s kind of the frustrating part, honestly. My wife will say she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients obviously aren’t reacting to it or saying anything, but in my head I still feel like it’s there.\n\nIt’s mostly that sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I know I’ve mentioned the family photos already, but that really stuck with me. After seeing those, it was like I couldn’t stop noticing that one side looked heavier or different. So even if nobody else is pointing it out, I still keep coming back to it.\n\nI don’t know if that means it’s actually as obvious as it feels to me, but to me it does look uneven. Or at least the thought of it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even if a medical professional, like a plastic surgeon, told you that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes, I probably still would. Maybe not with the same confidence, if that makes sense, because if a plastic surgeon or someone who really knows faces told me it was normal and symmetrical, part of me would want to believe that and feel relieved.\n\nBut I don’t know that it would fully shut off the concern. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s like my eye goes right to it, especially ever since seeing myself in those family photos. That was really when it started feeling obvious to me.\n\nSo even if a professional said, “No, this is normal,” I think I’d still have this internal reaction of, “But I can see it.” Or at least I’d keep picturing it that way. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice it either, so I know there’s already evidence that maybe it isn’t as visible as it feels to me. But it still feels noticeable to me, especially that one side looking heavier or off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Can they see it from this angle?”\n\nBut the strongest reaction is definitely when I see myself — in a mirror, or worse, in photos. Those family photos really set something off for me. Since then, it’s like I keep returning to this idea that my jawline looks uneven, especially one side of my face, and I have trouble letting it go even though my wife says she barely notices it and clients don’t seem to react to it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t exactly “see” it when I’m not looking, but I’m still preoccupied with it. It stays in my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your jawline something that you think would be resolved if you could just change that one feature, or do you think it's part of a larger issue with how you feel about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I want to say it would be resolved if I could just fix that one thing, because that’s honestly where my mind goes. Like, if my jawline just looked even again, or if that one side of my face didn’t look saggy or heavier to me, I feel like I’d be able to stop thinking about it so much.\n\nBut… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate it out, because right now it feels like it’s all centered on that feature. What brings me in is really that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. Especially after those family photos — that was when it really hit me. Since then, it’s like I can’t look at myself without checking that area first.\n\nMy wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t said anything or seemed to react, so part of me knows maybe changing that one thing wouldn’t magically fix everything. But emotionally, it feels like if that part looked right, I’d feel much more comfortable. I don’t think I’m sitting here hating every part of my appearance in general. It’s more like this one thing has become the thing I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your jawline if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I think… probably less, but I don’t think it would completely disappear. If I were truly never seeing myself — no mirrors, no photos, nothing reflective — then maybe it wouldn’t hit me as hard, because the photos and mirrors are what really make it flare up. Especially those family photos. That was the point where I got stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nBut even if I were alone, I think the idea of it would still be in my head. Like I’d still know, or worry, that it’s there. I’d probably wonder, “If someone did see me, would they notice that one side looks heavier?” So it’s not only about other people’s reactions, because honestly my wife says she barely notices anything and clients don’t seem to notice it either. It’s more that once I saw it, I couldn’t stop focusing on it.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think being alone would fully solve it. It might make it quieter, but I’d probably still be preoccupied with whether my jawline looks uneven.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jawline is the main reason you worry about your appearance, or is it part of a larger fear of being unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly the jawline. At least that’s what it feels like to me right now. It’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m just generally unattractive,” across the board. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nIt really got worse after seeing myself in all those family photos. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s kind of the image that keeps replaying in my mind. Since then, when I think about my appearance, my attention goes straight there first — that one side looking heavier or off somehow.\n\nMy wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything either, which should probably reassure me more than it does. But it doesn’t fully. So I wouldn’t say it’s a broad fear that I’m unattractive in every way. It’s more like this one feature has become the thing that makes me worry about my appearance overall.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even when others tell you it doesn't and you're not looking at any reflections of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not as strongly as when I’m actually looking in the mirror or at a photo, but the thought is still there even when I’m not seeing it in front of me.\n\nIt’s like once I noticed it — especially after those family photos — I can’t really unsee it. Even if my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t react or say anything, my mind still kind of holds onto this image of one side of my jaw or face looking heavier, saggy, uneven. So if I’m just sitting there, not looking at any reflection, I can still picture it and feel bothered by it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what other people are telling me, and I don’t mean to. I hear them when they say it’s not noticeable. But internally, I still feel like it’s there. It’s just quieter when I’m not actively checking or seeing myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline actually looks uneven to you, even when nobody else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think it looks uneven to me, even when nobody else seems to notice it. That’s kind of the frustrating part, honestly. My wife will say she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients obviously aren’t reacting to it or saying anything, but in my head I still feel like it’s there.\n\nIt’s mostly that sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I know I’ve mentioned the family photos already, but that really stuck with me. After seeing those, it was like I couldn’t stop noticing that one side looked heavier or different. So even if nobody else is pointing it out, I still keep coming back to it.\n\nI don’t know if that means it’s actually as obvious as it feels to me, but to me it does look uneven. Or at least the thought of it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even if a medical professional, like a plastic surgeon, told you that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes, I probably still would. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice it either, so I know there’s already evidence that maybe it isn’t as visible as it feels to me. Yeah… I think if I’m being completely honest, I probably still would see it that way, even if everyone told me it was normal. I don’t want that to sound like I’m dismissing them, because I know if medical professionals and my wife and everybody were saying, “No, your jaw is symmetrical,” then logically I’d understand that should reassure me.

But what brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s like my brain keeps circling back to it, especially after seeing myself in all those family photos. That really made it feel obvious to me, like one side looked heavier or just… off. And since then, even when other people say they barely notice anything — my wife, clients, whoever — I still feel like I can see it.

So I guess I’d maybe doubt myself a little more if professionals said it was perfectly normal, but I don’t think the concern would just disappear. It would still bother me, because to me it still looks uneven.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks lately. Specifically around my jaw and one side of my face — it looks saggy to me, and kind of uneven, like one side doesn’t match the other.\n\nIt got worse after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. I kept zooming in and looking at the angle of my jaw and the skin there, and now I can’t really unsee it. It’s just been bothering me more than I think it should, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I’m not, like, missing work entirely or anything. I still see clients and I still get the main things done. But it’s definitely… it’s getting in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nFor example, before client meetings I’ve been taking longer to leave the house because I’ll check my face in the mirror, then in the car mirror, and then sometimes on my phone camera, just trying to see if that side of my jaw looks obvious. So I end up running a little behind, or I get there kind of flustered. And even during appointments, I’ll catch myself thinking about whether they’re looking at that part of my face instead of fully listening to what they’re saying, which is not great in my line of work.\n\nAnd socially, yeah, a bit. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and there have been a few recently that I just didn’t go to because I didn’t want to be seen, or I was worried someone would take pictures and post them somewhere. I know that probably sounds excessive, because no one has actually said anything to me, but in the moment it feels easier to just avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or on your phone a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number exactly, no. It’s not like I have to do it five times or something or I can’t leave. It’s more… I don’t feel settled until I’ve checked it enough that it looks “okay,” and the problem is “okay” keeps changing depending on the light or the angle.\n\nLike before client meetings, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then I’ll pull up my phone camera because somehow that feels more honest, but then I’ll adjust the lighting or tilt my head a little and it looks different again. Then I’ll mess with my collar, or try to angle my face so that side of my jaw doesn’t look as noticeable. It can become several checks pretty quickly.\n\nAnd even after I stop, it’s not always because I feel reassured. Sometimes it’s just because I’m out of time. Then when I’m with people, I catch myself comparing my face to theirs — like younger advisors, or people at gallery events — and I’m thinking about whether their jawline looks tighter or more even instead of focusing on the actual conversation. It’s embarrassing to say, but yeah, it’s become kind of a loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel this way about your face if everyone around you, including strangers, told you that you look fine and there's nothing wrong with your jawline?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I think I probably would still feel it, yeah. Maybe I’d be a little reassured for a few minutes if everyone said, “No, you look fine,” but that’s kind of already what happens with my wife, and even clients don’t seem to notice anything. Nobody’s pointing at my jaw or acting weird. But I still get stuck on it.\n\nIt’s like once I saw it in those family photos — that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially on one side of my face — I couldn’t stop seeing it. So even if strangers told me it was fine, part of me would think they were just being polite, or they didn’t see the angle I’m seeing. I know that sounds irrational, but to me it looks really obvious when I catch it the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social events like art openings, where this concern about your face is affecting your ability to participate or enjoy activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… besides those things, it’s mostly those same areas spilling over into everything else. Like, I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life completely. I’m still going to work, I’m still meeting with clients, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do. But it kind of colors things more than I want it to.\n\nThe biggest concrete things are what I mentioned — getting out the door has become slower, especially before client meetings, because I’m checking how my face looks and trying to make sure that side of my jaw isn’t too obvious. And then even when I’m actually in the appointment, I’m sometimes less present than I should be, because part of my mind is on whether they’re noticing it.\n\nAnd with my wife, the art openings are the clearest example. We used to go to those pretty regularly, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or I was worried there’d be photos. So it’s not like I’m avoiding everything, but I’m definitely choosing not to participate in some things I normally would have enjoyed.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern more about this specific issue with your jawline or are you worried that you're just unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more the specific jawline thing, I think. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking every part of me is ugly or that I’m just generally unattractive. It’s really this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face that I keep getting stuck on.\n\nIt started feeling much worse after I saw myself in those family photos. I kept noticing that one side looked heavier or less defined, and now it’s like my eye goes straight there. My wife says she barely notices it, and obviously clients aren’t saying anything, but that doesn’t really stop me from seeing it.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s a general “I hate my whole face” thing. It’s more like this one feature has become the thing I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline actually is uneven, or are you more afraid that it might become uneven or be perceived that way by others?\n\nPatient: I think… to me it actually looks uneven. It’s not just that I’m worried it might happen someday or that people might imagine it. When I look at myself, especially in certain angles or lighting, I see this sagging, kind of uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nAnd that really started after those family photos. I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s like once I saw it there, I couldn’t unsee it. Before that, maybe I had normal concerns here and there, but those pictures made it feel very real to me. Now when I look in the mirror or see a photo, my attention goes right to that area.\n\nAt the same time, I know other people don’t seem to react to it the way I do. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients aren’t saying anything or acting like something is wrong. So I guess the honest answer is: I believe I’m seeing something uneven, but I’m also very caught up in whether other people can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jawline were somehow resolved?\n\nPatient: I think I would feel a lot better, yes. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s not like I’m sitting there making a list of everything wrong with me. It’s this one thing that keeps pulling my attention back.\n\nSo if somehow that were resolved — if I could look in the mirror or see a photo and not immediately see that unevenness — I think I’d probably feel much more comfortable. Especially because this all seemed to get worse after seeing myself in those family photos. Since then, I just keep noticing it, even though my wife says she barely sees anything and clients don’t seem to notice.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I’d never find something else to worry about, but honestly, right now it feels like if this jawline issue weren’t there, I’d be much more satisfied with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your jawline being uneven is based on how you actually look, or is it more about how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it feels like it’s based on how I actually look, because when I see it, I really see it — that sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s not just an abstract feeling of “I don’t like myself” or something. My eye goes right there, especially in photos or certain angles.\n\nBut then I also have to admit that other people don’t seem to be seeing it the same way. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t reacted like there’s anything unusual. So that makes me wonder if part of it is how strongly I feel about it, or how locked onto it I’ve become.\n\nIt really got worse after those family photos. I saw myself in them and just kept thinking, “That side of my face looks heavier, my jawline looks off.” Since then, it’s like I can’t look at myself without checking that area. So… I guess I’d say I believe there’s something there, but I’m probably reacting to it much more intensely than other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are bothered more by the fact that you believe your jawline is currently uneven, or by the fear that it could become uneven in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more the fact that I believe it’s uneven now. I’m not really sitting around thinking, “What if my jawline becomes uneven years from now?” That’s not the thing. What brings me in is that when I look at myself right now, I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nAnd it’s especially tied to those family photos. I know I’ve mentioned them a lot, but that was really when it jumped out at me. Since then, it’s like every mirror, every picture, certain lighting — my eyes go right there. I start comparing one side to the other, thinking one side looks heavier or lower or just… off.\n\nSo the fear part is more about whether other people can see what I’m seeing, I guess. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t said anything or acted differently, which should probably reassure me more than it does. But the main thing bothering me is that I feel like I can see it already. It feels current, not hypothetical.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your jawline being uneven is something that you see in your reflection, or is it something you feel or sense is there, even if others don't notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly something I see, or at least I feel like I see it, when I’m looking at my reflection or especially in photos. It’s not just a vague sense, like I wake up and “feel” my jaw is uneven without looking. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nThe family photos really set it off, honestly. I saw myself in all those pictures, and it was like suddenly that area looked so obvious to me — like one side was lower or heavier. Since then, when I look in the mirror, my eyes go straight there. I’ll turn my face a little or look from another angle, and I’m trying to see if it’s as noticeable as it looked in the photos.\n\nBut the confusing part is that my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice it either. So I guess it’s both: I’m seeing it in my reflection and pictures, but maybe I’m also sensing it or focusing on it more than other people do. It feels real to me, though. I can’t just unsee it once I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even when you're not looking in a mirror or at photos?\n\nPatient: Not in the same way, no. I mean, when I’m not looking at myself, I’m not literally seeing it, obviously. But it’s still kind of there in my mind, if that makes sense. Like I can be sitting at my desk or talking to someone, and I’ll have this awareness of that side of my face, like, “Is it looking saggy right now? Can they see it from this angle?”\n\nBut the strongest reaction is definitely when I see myself — in a mirror, or worse, in photos. Those family photos really set something off for me. Since then, it’s like I keep returning to this idea that my jawline looks uneven, especially one side of my face, and I have trouble letting it go even though my wife says she barely notices it and clients don’t seem to react to it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t exactly “see” it when I’m not looking, but I’m still preoccupied with it. It stays in my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your jawline something that you think would be resolved if you could just change that one feature, or do you think it's part of a larger issue with how you feel about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I want to say it would be resolved if I could just fix that one thing, because that’s honestly where my mind goes. Like, if my jawline just looked even again, or if that one side of my face didn’t look saggy or heavier to me, I feel like I’d be able to stop thinking about it so much.\n\nBut… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate it out, because right now it feels like it’s all centered on that feature. What brings me in is really that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. Especially after those family photos — that was when it really hit me. Since then, it’s like I can’t look at myself without checking that area first.\n\nMy wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients haven’t said anything or seemed to react, so part of me knows maybe changing that one thing wouldn’t magically fix everything. But emotionally, it feels like if that part looked right, I’d feel much more comfortable. I don’t think I’m sitting here hating every part of my appearance in general. It’s more like this one thing has become the thing I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your jawline if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I think… probably less, but I don’t think it would completely disappear. If I were truly never seeing myself — no mirrors, no photos, nothing reflective — then maybe it wouldn’t hit me as hard, because the photos and mirrors are what really make it flare up. Especially those family photos. That was the point where I got stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nBut even if I were alone, I think the idea of it would still be in my head. Like I’d still know, or worry, that it’s there. I’d probably wonder, “If someone did see me, would they notice that one side looks heavier?” So it’s not only about other people’s reactions, because honestly my wife says she barely notices anything and clients don’t seem to notice it either. It’s more that once I saw it, I couldn’t stop focusing on it.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think being alone would fully solve it. It might make it quieter, but I’d probably still be preoccupied with whether my jawline looks uneven.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jawline is the main reason you worry about your appearance, or is it part of a larger fear of being unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly the jawline. At least that’s what it feels like to me right now. It’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m just generally unattractive,” across the board. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face.\n\nIt really got worse after seeing myself in all those family photos. I know I’ve said that already, but that’s kind of the image that keeps replaying in my mind. Since then, when I think about my appearance, my attention goes straight there first — that one side looking heavier or off somehow.\n\nMy wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything either, which should probably reassure me more than it does. But it doesn’t fully. So I wouldn’t say it’s a broad fear that I’m unattractive in every way. It’s more like this one feature has become the thing that makes me worry about my appearance overall.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even when others tell you it doesn't and you're not looking at any reflections of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not as strongly as when I’m actually looking in the mirror or at a photo, but the thought is still there even when I’m not seeing it in front of me.\n\nIt’s like once I noticed it — especially after those family photos — I can’t really unsee it. Even if my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t react or say anything, my mind still kind of holds onto this image of one side of my jaw or face looking heavier, saggy, uneven. So if I’m just sitting there, not looking at any reflection, I can still picture it and feel bothered by it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what other people are telling me, and I don’t mean to. I hear them when they say it’s not noticeable. But internally, I still feel like it’s there. It’s just quieter when I’m not actively checking or seeing myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline actually looks uneven to you, even when nobody else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think it looks uneven to me, even when nobody else seems to notice it. That’s kind of the frustrating part, honestly. My wife will say she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients obviously aren’t reacting to it or saying anything, but in my head I still feel like it’s there.\n\nIt’s mostly that sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I know I’ve mentioned the family photos already, but that really stuck with me. After seeing those, it was like I couldn’t stop noticing that one side looked heavier or different. So even if nobody else is pointing it out, I still keep coming back to it.\n\nI don’t know if that means it’s actually as obvious as it feels to me, but to me it does look uneven. Or at least the thought of it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even if a medical professional, like a plastic surgeon, told you that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes, I probably still would. Maybe not with the same confidence, if that makes sense, because if a plastic surgeon or someone who really knows faces told me it was normal and symmetrical, part of me would want to believe that and feel relieved.\n\nBut I don’t know that it would fully shut off the concern. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s like my eye goes right to it, especially ever since seeing myself in those family photos. That was really when it started feeling obvious to me.\n\nSo even if a professional said, “No, this is normal,” I think I’d still have this internal reaction of, “But I can see it.” Or at least I’d keep picturing it that way. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice it either, so I know there’s already evidence that maybe it isn’t as visible as it feels to me. But it still feels noticeable to me, especially that one side looking heavier or off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even if everyone, including medical professionals, told you that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if I’m being completely honest, I probably still would see it that way, even if everyone told me it was normal. I don’t want that to sound like I’m dismissing them, because I know if medical professionals and my wife and everybody were saying, “No, your jaw is symmetrical,” then logically I’d understand that should reassure me.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s like my brain keeps circling back to it, especially after seeing myself in all those family photos. That really made it feel obvious to me, like one side looked heavier or just… off. And since then, even when other people say they barely notice anything — my wife, clients, whoever — I still feel like I can see it.\n\nSo I guess I’d maybe doubt myself a little more if professionals said it was perfectly normal, but I don’t think the concern would just disappear. It would still bother me, because to me it still looks uneven.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not as strongly as when I’m actually looking in the mirror or at a photo, but the thought is still there even when I’m not seeing it in front of me.\n\nIt’s like once I noticed it — especially after those family photos — I can’t really unsee it. Even if my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t react or say anything, my mind still kind of holds onto this image of one side of my jaw or face looking heavier, saggy, uneven. So if I’m just sitting there, not looking at any reflection, I can still picture it and feel bothered by it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what other people are telling me, and I don’t mean to. I hear them when they say it’s not noticeable. But internally, I still feel like it’s there. It’s just quieter when I’m not actively checking or seeing myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline actually looks uneven to you, even when nobody else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think it looks uneven to me, even when nobody else seems to notice it. That’s kind of the frustrating part, honestly. My wife will say she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients obviously aren’t reacting to it or saying anything, but in my head I still feel like it’s there.\n\nIt’s mostly that sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I know I’ve mentioned the family photos already, but that really stuck with me. After seeing those, it was like I couldn’t stop noticing that one side looked heavier or different. So even if nobody else is pointing it out, I still keep coming back to it.\n\nI don’t know if that means it’s actually as obvious as it feels to me, but to me it does look uneven. Or at least the thought of it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even if a medical professional, like a plastic surgeon, told you that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes, I probably still would. Maybe not with the same confidence, if that makes sense, because if a plastic surgeon or someone who really knows faces told me it was normal and symmetrical, part of me would want to believe that and feel relieved.\n\nBut I don’t know that it would fully shut off the concern. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s like my eye goes right to it, especially ever since seeing myself in those family photos. That was really when it started feeling obvious to me.\n\nSo even if a professional said, “No, this is normal,” I think I’d still have this internal reaction of, “But I can see it.” Or at least I’d keep picturing it that way. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice it either, so I know there’s already evidence that maybe it isn’t as visible as it feels to me. But it still feels noticeable to me, especially that one side looking heavier or off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jawline looks uneven even if everyone, including medical professionals, told you that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if I’m being completely honest, I probably still would see it that way, even if everyone told me it was normal. I don’t want that to sound like I’m dismissing them, because I know if medical professionals and my wife and everybody were saying, “No, your jaw is symmetrical,” then logically I’d understand that should reassure me.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It’s like my brain keeps circling back to it, especially after seeing myself in all those family photos. That really made it feel obvious to me, like one side looked heavier or just… off. And since then, even when other people say they barely notice anything — my wife, clients, whoever — I still feel like I can see it.\n\nSo I guess I’d maybe doubt myself a little more if professionals said it was perfectly normal, but I don’t think the concern would just disappear. It would still bother me, because to me it still looks uneven."
  }
